Conclusion and Related Work

We have proposed new classes of constraints that are designed to capture the complex, higher-order behaviours of programs with first-class procedures.
We developed their theory to (a) show decidability of the classes and (b) situate them with respect to higher-order program verification.
We also described an implementation and its application to the verification of socket programming.

Complexity.

Our reduction of intersection typeability to
MSL
(
\omega
) satisfiability gives us
(n-1)
-EXPTIME hardness of
MSL
(
\omega
) satisfiability.
Furthermore, the reduction of order-
n

MSL
(
\omega
) to order-
(n+1)
HORS with cases provides an
(n+1)
-EXPTIME upper bound, thanks to a result by
Clairambault et al. 2018
.
We derive this same naive upper bound directly from the decision procedure, where every application of
(Step)
or
(Assm)
on an order-
n
symbol has
n
-exponentially many candidate side conditions.
Further study is required to obtain a tighter upper bound.

Related work

We survey some of the work that is most closely related to our own.

Automata, types, and clauses.

MSL was proposed independently by
Weidenbach 1999
and
Nielson
et al. 2002
(as
\mathcal{H}1
), with
Goubault-Larrecq 2005
providing the bridge between the two.
Since then, it has been extended beyond Horn and with the addition of straight dismatching constraints in
Teucke and
Weidenbach 2017
.
Recall that the solved form of clauses for the first-order
\mathcal{H}1
fragment were named
automaton
clauses because of their shape, a connection that has also been made in
Weidenbach 1999
;
Nagaya and Toyama 2002
.
This name is equally justified for our (higher-order) automaton clauses, since they, too, define finite tree automata
(
Broadbent and
Kobayashi 2013
, via intersection types,)
.
The relationship between higher-order automata, types, and particular sets of clauses goes back to
Frühwirth et al. 1997
.

Set constraints

Set constraints are a powerful language that has been very influential in program analysis
(
Aiken 1999
)
.
They are known to be equivalent to the monadic class
(
Bachmair
et al. 1993
)
and, therefore, have a very close connection with
MSL
.
Higher-order set constraints have also been considered, defining sets of terms rather than higher-order predicates much like
\textsf{MSL}(\omega)

(
Goubault-Larrecq 2002b
)
.
Although the relationship between our constraints and those of
loc cit
is not well understood, we point out that their constraints are solvable in
2
-NEXPTIME, whereas satisfiability in our class is
(n-1)
-EXPTIME hard.

HORS model checking

There is a strong connection between traditional higher-order model checking with higher-order recursion schemes
(
Kobayashi 2013
, e.g.)
and
\textsf{MSL}(\omega)
problems, as witnessed by their interreducibility.
Many approaches to inferring and verifying types for higher-order recursion schemes have been considered, but the most closely related to our work is the saturation-based approach considered by
Broadbent and
Kobayashi 2013
.
The main novelty of their algorithm is that typing constraints are propagated backwards starting from the final (unaccepted) states, rather than the forward from the target state.
While backward propagation is analogous to goal-orientation search, attempting to derive clauses in order to rewrite the goal, their saturation-based approach is similar to our accumulation of automaton clauses in a bottom-up manner.
Furthermore, follow up work improved upon the efficiency of the saturation-based approach by representing intersection types as a type of binary decision diagrams that compactly describes a family of sets
(
Terao and
Kobayashi 2014
)
.
More work needs to be done to draw a detailed comparison between these algorithms and our own.
Furthermore, as many of these algorithms are in their second or third generation, there will be possible optimisations that can be transferred to our own setting, in addition to novel optimisations that take advantage of our setting.

HFL model checking

Higher-order fixed-point logic, HFL, is a very expressive logic also designed as an appropriate language for program verification
(
Kobayashi 2021
)
.
It is more expressive than higher-order constrained Horn clauses in general, and our fragment in particular, by supporting both the greatest and least fixed-point.
This duality allows it to express liveness properties as well as safety properties.
Furthermore, this logic allows for a background constraint theory.
In the pure case, HFL is known to be decidable by reduction to intersection typing problem
(
Hosoi
et al. 2019
)
.

Refinement type checking and constrained Horn clauses

It was observed by
Grebenshchikov et al. 2012
that a standard approach taken to solving refinement type inference problems, such as
Jhala
et al. 2011
;
Unno and
Kobayashi 2009
;
Terauchi 2010
, is essentially a reduction to constrained Horn clause solving.
Although only first-order, these systems of constraints are extremely expressive since they incorporate an arbitrary background theory, such as linear arithmetic or the theory of algebraic datatypes.
Consequently, they are typically undecidable.
Constrained Horn clauses were lifted to higher-order by
Cathcart Burn
et al. 2017
, and the theory further explored in
Ong and Wagner 2019
.
In a follow-up work the same authors identified a family of decidable fragments intended for applications in database aggregation
(
Cathcart Burn et al. 2021
)
.

Uniform proofs and logic programming.

The formulation of our fragments and the proof system that underlies them follows the elegant presentation in the work of Miller and his collaborators, such as
Miller et al. 1991
;
Miller and
Nadathur 2012
. In particular, one can recognise their
fohc
,
hohc
, and
hohh
as the underlying formalisms behind our
\textsf{MSL}(1)
clauses,
\textsf{HOMSL}(\omega)
clauses, and higher-order automaton clauses respectively. Of course, we could have presented our fragments of HOL in a more traditional format for automated reasoning (e.g. with clauses as multisets of literals), but we consider the compositional characterisation that is characteristic of Miller’s work essential for a clear exposition once we have to deal with the combination of nested clauses (in the sense of hereditary Harrop) and higher-order constructs.

Constructive logic and ‘Horn Clauses as Types’

Over a series of papers, Fu, Komendantskaya, and co-authors have presented a comprehensive analysis of Horn clauses and resolution according to the propositions-as-types tradition
(
Fu and
Komendantskaya 2015
;
Fu
et al. 2016
;
Fu and
Komendantskaya 2017
;
Farka 2020
)
.
Like our work, they cast resolution as a form of rewriting, studying a number of different variations on the standard approach that have been motivated by the desire to capture computations with infinite data. Using Howard’s System
H

(
Howard 1980
)
, they give a type theoretic semantics to each form of resolution, and this allows for a more meaningful notion of soundness and completeness than the traditional method using Herbrand models. Since it is in the propositions-as-types tradition, their work views a Horn clause as the type of its proofs. By contrast, we view an automaton clause with a single free variable
x
as a type inhabited by the terms that satisfy the clause (when substituted for
x
). Consequently, we do not make use of the constructive content of the resolution proofs themselves, but rather view resolution simply as a mechanism for generating a new clause from two given clauses – i.e. a way to infer new types.

Acknowledgements.
We gratefully acknowledge the support of the Sponsor Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council
http://https://epsrc.ukri.org
(Grant #EP/T006595/1) and the National Centre for Cyber Security via the UK Research Institute in Verified Trustworthy Software Systems.
We are also very grateful for the help of the reviewers in making the paper more clear and accurate, and for suggesting related work.

Appendix A
Semantics

Our fragments use the standard semantics of HOL.
An explicit denotational semantics is provided below.

Interpretation of types.

Let
\Sigma
be a constructor signature.
A
\Sigma
-
structure

\mathcal{A}
assigns a non-empty set
A_{\iota}
to the semantic domain of
\iota
and an element
c^{a}\in\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket
to each constructor
a:\gamma\in\Sigma
, with types interpreted as:

\llbracket\iota\rrbracket:=A_{\iota}\qquad\llbracket o\rrbracket:=\mathbb{B}\qquad\llbracket\sigma_{1}\to\sigma_{2}\rrbracket:=\left[\llbracket\sigma_{1}\rrbracket\to\llbracket\sigma_{2}\rrbracket\right]

where
\mathbb{B}
denotes the boolean lattice and
\left[\llbracket\sigma_{1}\rrbracket\to\llbracket\sigma_{2}\rrbracket\right]
denotes the full function space between
\llbracket\sigma_{1}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\sigma_{2}\rrbracket
, as dictated by the standard semantics of HOL.

Interpretation of terms and goal formulas.

Let
\Pi^{\prime}
consist of predicate signature
\Pi
and a variable environment
\Delta
.
A
\Pi^{\prime}
-
valuation
(typically
\alpha
) is a function such that
\alpha(x)\in\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket
, for all
x:\sigma\in\Pi^{\prime}
(taking the liberty to write
x
for both variables and predicates in
\Pi^{\prime}
.)

By some abuse, we consider goal formulas as goal
terms
, treating logical symbols as constants with their usual interpretations from HOL:

\displaystyle\llbracket x:\sigma\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\alpha(x)

\displaystyle\llbracket a:\gamma\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=c^{a}

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{true}:o\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\llbracket s\,t:\sigma_{2}\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\llbracket s:\sigma_{1}\to\sigma_{2}\rrbracket(\alpha)(\llbracket t:\sigma_{1}\rrbracket(\alpha))

\displaystyle\llbracket\lambda x:\sigma_{1}.\,t:\sigma_{2}\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lambda s\in\llbracket\sigma_{1}\rrbracket.\,\llbracket t:\sigma_{2}\rrbracket(\alpha[x\mapsto s])

\displaystyle\llbracket\wedge:o\to o\to o\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lambda b_{1}\,b_{2}.\,\min\{b_{1},b_{2}\}

\displaystyle\llbracket\exists_{\sigma}:(\sigma\to o)\to o\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lambda r.\,\max\{r\,s\mid s\in\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket\}

where
\sigma
is either a constructor type
\gamma
or predicate type
\rho
, and the
\Sigma
-structure
\mathcal{A}
and environment
\Pi^{\prime}
are left implicit.

Interpretation of clauses.

Interpreting clauses requires additional constants, again with their standard semantics.
Since the types are clear, we omit them.

\displaystyle\llbracket\vee\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lambda b_{1}\,b_{2}.\,\max\{b_{1},b_{2}\}

\displaystyle\llbracket\neg\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lambda b.\,1-b

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Rightarrow}\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lambda b_{1}\,b_{2}.\,\llbracket\lor\rrbracket\,(\llbracket\neg\rrbracket\,b_{1})\,b_{2}

\displaystyle\llbracket\forall_{\sigma}\rrbracket(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lambda r.\,\min\{r\,s\mid s\in\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket\}

We have allowed some redundancy in the logical symbols for convenience:
the semantics of
\forall_{\sigma}
can equivalently be formulated in terms of
\neg
and
\exists_{\sigma}
, and
\lor
in terms of
\neg
and
\land
.

The above determines the semantics of clauses-treated-as-terms.
This behaves as expected, with the semantics
\llbracket\forall\overline{{y}}.\,G\Rightarrow A\rrbracket(\alpha)
of definite clause
\forall\overline{{y}}.\,G\Rightarrow A
given by:

\displaystyle\min\left\{\max\{\llbracket A\rrbracket(\alpha[\overline{{y}}\mapsto\overline{{s}}]),1-\llbracket G\rrbracket(\alpha[\overline{{y}}\mapsto\overline{{s}}])\}\;\middle|\;\vphantom{\bigcup}\forall y_{i}\in\overline{{y}}.\,s_{i}\in\llbracket\sigma_{i}\rrbracket\right\}

I.e. a clause valuates to
0
iff there exists a valuation of the top-level universal variables such that the body valuates to
1
and the head to
0
.

Satisfaction.

A model of
D
over
\Sigma
and
\Pi
consists of a
\Sigma
-structure
\mathcal{A}
and a
\Pi
-valuation
\alpha
such that
\llbracket D\rrbracket(\alpha)=1
.
We say
D\vDash G
just if
\llbracket G\rrbracket(\alpha)=1
for all models of
D
.

A.1.
Completeness of the proof system

This section demonstrates soundness and completeness of the Figure
2
proof system with respect to the standard semantics of HOL.

See
2.4

Soundness follows from each rule being clearly admissible in HOL.

For completeness, suppose
D\models G
over constructor signature
\Sigma
and predicate signature
\Pi
.
We assume
D
contains a universal clause
(\forall\overline{{y}}.\,\mathsf{true}\Rightarrow\mathsf{Univ}_{\rho}\,\overline{{y}})
for every type
\rho
occurring in
D
or
G
.
We refer to
\mathsf{Univ}_{\rho}
as a
universal relation
.

We construct an no-instance of the HOCHC satisfiability problem over the equational theory of first-order trees as background.
The instance is constructed by defunctionalising the higher-order constructors and simulating both pattern-matching clause heads and the higher-order typing discipline on first-order trees with equational constraints.

The HOCHC signature.

Types
\tau
are transformed to HOCHC types
\langle\!|\tau|\!\rangle
:

\langle\!|\gamma|\!\rangle:=\iota\qquad\langle\!|o|\!\rangle:=o\qquad\langle\!|\sigma\to\rho|\!\rangle:=\langle\!|\sigma|\!\rangle\to\langle\!|\rho|\!\rangle

We flatten our constructors to first-order, where the only non-nullary constructor is binary application, written
\_\cdot{}\_
.
A family of new unary predicate symbols
T_{\gamma}
is axiomatised so as to restrict
x:\gamma
to represent a well-formed tree of type
\gamma
under the original higher-order signature
\Sigma
.

Let
\mathsf{sorts}_{\exists}(D\land G)
contain all types of existential variables in
D\land G
, so we can define first-order signature
\Sigma_{\mathsf{FO}}
and set of predicates
\Delta
.

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\mathsf{FO}}

\displaystyle:=\{f:\iota\mid f:\gamma\in\Sigma\}\cup\{\_\cdot{}\_:\iota\to\iota\to\iota\}

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle:=\Pi\cup\{T_{\gamma}:\iota\to o\mid\gamma\in\mathsf{sorts}_{\exists}(D\land G)\lor(f:\gamma_{1}\to\dots\to\gamma_{n}\in\Sigma\land\gamma\in\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\})\}

The transformation.

We define a term transformation
\langle\!|t|\!\rangle
by:

\langle\!|x|\!\rangle:=x\qquad\langle\!|c|\!\rangle:=c\qquad\langle\!|P|\!\rangle:=P\qquad\langle\!|s\,t|\!\rangle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\langle\!|s|\!\rangle\cdot\langle\!|t|\!\rangle&\text{if }s\,t:\gamma\\
\langle\!|s|\!\rangle\,\langle\!|t|\!\rangle&\text{o/w}\end{array}\right.

We extend this to goal formulas
\langle\!|G|\!\rangle
by:

\displaystyle\langle\!|\mathsf{true}|\!\rangle

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{true}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\langle\!|\exists x:\rho.\,G|\!\rangle

\displaystyle:=\exists x:\langle\!|\rho|\!\rangle.\,\langle\!|G|\!\rangle

\displaystyle\langle\!|G\wedge H|\!\rangle

\displaystyle:=\langle\!|G|\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!|H|\!\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\langle\!|\exists x:\gamma.\,G|\!\rangle

\displaystyle:=\exists x:\iota.\,T_{\gamma}\,x\land\langle\!|G|\!\rangle

We extend to definite formulas
\langle\!|C|\!\rangle
by:

\displaystyle\langle\!|\mathsf{true}|\!\rangle

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{true}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\langle\!|\forall\overline{{y}}.\,G\Rightarrow P\,\overline{{y}}|\!\rangle

\displaystyle:=\forall\overline{{y}}.\,\langle\!|G|\!\rangle\Rightarrow P\,\overline{{y}}

\displaystyle\langle\!|C\wedge D|\!\rangle

\displaystyle:=\langle\!|C|\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!|D|\!\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\langle\!|\forall\overline{{y}}.\,G\Rightarrow P\,(c\,\overline{{y}})|\!\rangle

\displaystyle:=\forall z.\,\langle\!|G|\!\rangle_{z=c\overline{{y}}}\Rightarrow P\,z

where

\langle\!|G|\!\rangle_{z=c\overline{{y}}}:=\exists\overline{{y}}.\,z=\langle\!|c\,\overline{{y}}|\!\rangle\land\bigwedge_{y_{i}:\gamma_{i}\in\overline{{y}}}T_{\gamma_{i}}\,y_{i}\land\langle\!|G|\!\rangle

Finally, we define an equi-satisfiable HOCHC instance over the first-order equational theory of trees with signature
\Sigma_{\mathsf{FO}}
and predicates
\Delta
:

\displaystyle D_{\mathit{Th}}

\displaystyle:=\langle\!|D|\!\rangle\land\{\forall x.\,\langle\!|\mathsf{true}|\!\rangle_{x=fy_{1}\dots y_{n}}\Rightarrow T_{\gamma}\,x\mid f:\gamma_{1}\to\dots\to\gamma_{n}\to\gamma\in\Sigma\}

\displaystyle G_{\mathit{Th}}

\displaystyle:=\langle\!|G|\!\rangle

The transformation
\langle\!|\_|\!\rangle
has a straightforward inverse
\langle\!|\_|\!\rangle^{-1}
on existential-free goal formulas.

Note that all universal variables are guarded by a
T_{\gamma}
of the appropriate type.
Since universal variables do not occur in goal formulas, those do not need to be guarded.

The HOCHC proof system.

Ong and Wagner 2019
propose a simple, sound and refutationally complete resolution proof system for HOCHC (under the standard semantics of HOL) that we shall call the
HOCHC proof system
.
Any resolution proof for our HOCHC instance requires only two of their three proof rules, due to the lack of internal
\lambda
s and the fact that the HOCHC proof system does not introduce those.
In the terminology of our paper, these two proof rules are:

\begin{array}[]{cc}\!\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 37.42061pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\textstyle\exists\overline{{y}}.\bigwedge_{i\in[1..n]}\varphi_{i}\land\exists\overline{{x}}.\bigwedge_{j\in[1..m]}x_{k_{j}}\,\overline{{M_{j}}}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-2.08893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 3.3806pt\hbox{\raise-1.1306pt\hbox{ {\small{(OW${}_{\top}$)}}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=150.5747pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-2.08893pt\hbox{\hskip 104.70795pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\top\mathstrut$}}}}$}\par\;\,\par\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 44.63005pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\exists\overline{{z}}.\,G\land P\,\overline{{M}}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-2.59784pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.75891pt\hbox{ {\small{(OW${}_{\text{R}}$)}}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.88951pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=72.86386pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.38951pt\hbox{ \!\!\!\! $(\forall\overline{{y}}.\,G^{\prime}\Rightarrow P\,\overline{{y}})\in D_{\mathit{Th}}$ }}}}}\vbox{\vskip-2.59784pt\hbox{\hskip 35.42274pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\exists\overline{{z}}.\,G\land G^{\prime}[\overline{{M}}/\overline{{y}}]\mathstrut$}}}}$}\end{array}

where
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}
are constraints from the equational theory of trees for which there exists a satisfying assignment
\alpha
(i.e.
\alpha\vDash\varphi_{1}\land\dots\land\varphi_{n}
such that
\alpha(y)
is a finite tree over
\Sigma_{\mathsf{FO}}
, for all
y\in\overline{{y}}
).
Note that
\overline{{x}}
in
(OW
⊤
)
are higher-order and
\overline{{y}}
first-order existentials.
Proof rule
(OW
⊤
)
implicitly assigns universal relations to
\overline{{x}}
;
these are part of the semantic domain and guaranteed to satisfy the formula.

We write
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime\prime}
to denote
G^{\prime}
as a premise for
G^{\prime\prime}
in the HOCHC proof system, with
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}G^{\prime\prime}
its reflexive, transitive closure.

Notation 0.

Every HOCHC goal formula
G^{\prime}
can be written as
\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}.\,G_{\varphi}\land G_{\mathsf{var}}\land G_{A}
where:

•

G_{\varphi}
is existential-free and contains the constraints and atoms headed by
T_{\gamma}
,

•

G_{\mathsf{var}}
is existential-free and contains the atoms headed by
x\in\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}
, and

•

G_{A}
is existential-free and contains the remaining atoms.

We may write
G_{\neg\varphi}
for
G_{\mathsf{var}}\land G_{A}
.

HOCHC semantics.

Recall that
D\vDash G
means “every model of
D
satisfies
G
”, much like
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vDash G_{\mathit{Th}}
means “every model of
D_{\mathit{Th}}
satisfies
G_{\mathit{Th}}
”.
Note, however, that a HOCHC instance has a fixed background theory from which it inherits
\llbracket\iota\rrbracket
and interpretations of the constants in
\Sigma
.
Intuitively, our quantifier ranges over all
\Pi
-valuations and
\Sigma
-structures, while the HOCHC quantifier only ranges over
\Pi
-valuations that extend the background theory.

Consequently, we can choose our
\Sigma
-structure in the HOCHC case to match the background theory of trees, and it is not difficult to see that
D\vDash G
implies
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vDash G_{\mathit{Th}}
.

Proof outline.

By refutation completeness of the HOCHC proof system, there exists a HOCHC proof
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash G_{\mathit{Th}}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}\top
with satisfying assignment
\alpha
to the first-order existentials.

We replay this HOCHC proof in our Figure
2
proof system starting from
G
.
Since all existential variables are guarded by typing predicates
T_{\gamma}
and all elements of the universe of the theory of (finite) trees are representable by terms, the satisfying assignment can be explicitly constructed and implemented using
(Ex)
.
Furthermore, the presence of universal clauses allow us to prove existential-headed atoms using
(Ex)
as well
7
7

7

Although the type of a HOCHC existential may not match the corresponding original existential, the existence of the appropriate universal clause in
D
is guaranteed.
.
Hence
D\vdash G
.

From constraints to type-respecting substitutions.

The purpose of our equality constraints and
T_{\gamma}
predicates is to restrict the domain of HOCHC variable
y:\iota
to well-formed trees of type
\gamma
under the original higher-order signature;
they guarantee that every satisfying assignment
\alpha
from an application of
(OW
⊤
)
respects the original higher-order types.

We say that an assignment
\alpha
to
\overline{{y}}
is
type-respecting
just if, for all
y\in\overline{{y}}
, there exists a closed tree
t:\gamma
over
\Sigma
such that
\alpha(y)=\langle\!|t|\!\rangle
and
\gamma
is the original type of
y
.

Lemma A.1.

If
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\exists y.\,T_{\gamma}\,y\twoheadrightarrow^{*}H\twoheadrightarrow\top
with satisfying assignment
\alpha
, then
\alpha
is type-respecting.

Proof.

Ong and Wagner 2019
leave existential quantifiers implicit in the HOCHC proof system.
For convenience, we do the same here.
We use a straightforward induction on length
k
in
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash T_{\gamma}\,y\twoheadrightarrow^{k}H\twoheadrightarrow\top
.

Clearly,
k
cannot be zero, so suppose
k=1
.
This first proof step must be
(OW
{}_{\text{R}}
)
with a side condition
(\forall x.\,x=\langle\!|f|\!\rangle\Rightarrow T_{\gamma}\,x)\in D_{\mathit{Th}}
with
f:\gamma\in\Sigma
, so
H=(y=\langle\!|f|\!\rangle)
.
This means that
y
equals
\langle\!|f|\!\rangle
under
\alpha
, so
\alpha(y)=\langle\!|f|\!\rangle
witnesses that
\alpha
is type-respecting.

Suppose
k>1
with side condition
(\forall x.\,\exists\overline{{z}}.\,x=\langle\!|f\,\overline{{z}}|\!\rangle\land\bigwedge_{j\in[1..m]}T_{\gamma_{j}}\,z_{j}\Rightarrow T_{\gamma}\,x)\in D_{\mathit{Th}}
with
f:\gamma_{1}\to\dots\to\gamma_{m}\to\gamma\in\Sigma
.
The result of this first step is
G^{\prime\prime}=(y=\langle\!|f\,\overline{{z}}|\!\rangle)\land\bigwedge_{j\in[1..m]}T_{\gamma_{j}}\,z_{j}
, still omitting quantifiers.

For all
j\in[1..m]
,
\alpha
is a satisfying assignment for some
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash T_{\gamma_{j}}\,z_{j}\twoheadrightarrow^{k_{j}}\bot
with
k_{j}<k
.
By the IH,
\alpha(z_{j})=\langle\!|t_{j}|\!\rangle
for closed tree
t_{j}:\gamma_{j}
over
\Sigma
.

Now,
\alpha
satisfies
H
and, by soundness of the HOCHC proof system, also
G^{\prime\prime}
.
In particular, this means
y
equals
\langle\!|f\,\overline{{z}}|\!\rangle
under
\alpha
, so
\alpha(y)
equals
\langle\!|f\,\overline{{t}}|\!\rangle
for closed tree
f\,\overline{{t}}:\gamma
over
\Sigma
, as required.
∎

Observe from the transformation
\langle\!|\_|\!\rangle
that all first-order existentials occurring in a HOCHC proof of a
\langle\!|G^{\prime}|\!\rangle
are guarded by the appropriate
T_{\gamma}
.
It is easy to see that the above lemma generalises to
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}.\,\bigwedge_{y_{i}:\gamma_{i}\in\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}T_{\gamma_{i}}\,y_{i}\land G^{0}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}H\twoheadrightarrow\top
with existential-free
G^{0}
.
Since every
\langle\!|G^{\prime}|\!\rangle
can be written in this shape, the following holds.

Lemma A.2.

If
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\langle\!|G^{\prime}|\!\rangle\twoheadrightarrow^{*}\top
with satisfying assignment
\alpha
, then
\alpha
is type-respecting.

Note that any satisfying assignment
\alpha
to
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}.\,G_{\varphi}\land G_{\neg\varphi}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}\top
is also a satisfying assignment to
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}.\,G_{\neg\varphi}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}\top
. We know that
\alpha
respects original types, so
[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}]
is a well-defined substitution in our original program, giving rise to the following lemma that allows us to replay a HOCHC proof in our Figure
2
proof system.

Lemma A.3 (Replay lemma).

If
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}.\,G_{\varphi}\land G_{\neg\varphi}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}\top
with satisfying assignment
\alpha
, then

D\vdash\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]

Proof.

Suppose
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}H\twoheadrightarrow\top
with satisfying assignment
\alpha
.
Because
(OW
{}_{\text{R}}
)
is a local rule and
(OW
⊤
)
a global one, and
T_{\gamma}
-clauses only have other
T_{\gamma^{\prime}}
-predicates in their bodies, we may assume WLOG that all
T_{\gamma}
-headed resolutions in a HOCHC proof are performed last.

Suppose our HOCHC proof has shape
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{k}G^{\prime\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{\ell}H\twoheadrightarrow\top
for a
G^{\prime\prime}
where the first
k
steps do not use
T_{\gamma}
-headed clauses, followed by
\ell
steps that (exclusively) do.
We use induction on
k
.

•

If
k=0
, then let
G^{\prime}=G^{\prime\prime}=\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}.\,G_{\varphi}\land G_{\mathsf{var}}
with
\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}:\langle\!|\overline{{\rho}}|\!\rangle
.
Let
G_{\mathsf{var}}=\bigwedge_{j\in[1..m]}x_{i_{j}}\,\overline{{M_{j}}}
with
x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{m}}\in\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}
.
We prove
D\vdash\langle\!|G_{\mathsf{var}}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]
where
\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}:\overline{{\rho}}
:

\displaystyle{\forall j\in[1..m].\!\!\!\!}\mathstrut

(T)

\displaystyle D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\mathsf{true}\mathstrut

(Res)

\displaystyle D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\mathsf{Univ}_{i_{j}}\,\langle\!|\overline{{M_{j}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]\mathstrut

(And)
∗

\displaystyle D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\bigwedge_{j\in[1..m]}\mathsf{Univ}_{i_{j}}\,\langle\!|\overline{{M_{j}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]\mathstrut

•

If
k>0
, then let
G^{\prime}=\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}.\,G_{\varphi}\land G_{\neg\varphi}\land P\,\overline{{s}}
such that the first step is
(OW
{}_{\text{R}}
)
on
P\,\overline{{s}}
.
Suppose
\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}
and
\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}
with
\rho
as in the previous case, and
\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}
and
\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}^{0}}}
with
\rho^{0}
analogous.

We shall abbreviate
[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{x_{1}}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{x_{1}}},\dots,\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{x_{n}}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{x_{n}}}]
to
[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{x_{1}}}\dots\overline{{x_{n}}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{x_{1}}}\dots\overline{{x_{n}}}]
.

–

Suppose the side condition is
(\forall\overline{{z}}.\,\langle\!|\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,G^{0}|\!\rangle\Rightarrow P\,\overline{{z}})\in D_{\mathit{Th}}
with existential-free
G^{0}
from some
(\forall\overline{{z}}.\,\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,G^{0}\Rightarrow P\,\overline{{z}})\in D
.
In this case,
\langle\!|\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,G^{0}|\!\rangle
equals some
\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,\bigwedge_{y_{j}^{0}\in\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}}T_{\gamma_{j}^{0}}\,y_{j}^{0}\land\langle\!|G^{0}|\!\rangle
.
This gives us:

G^{\prime\prime}=\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,G_{\varphi}\land G_{\neg\varphi}\land\bigwedge_{y_{j}^{0}\in\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}}T_{\gamma_{j}^{0}}\,y_{j}^{0}\land\langle\!|G^{0}|\!\rangle[\overline{{s}}/\overline{{z}}]

We aim to show
D\vdash\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}\land P\,\overline{{s}}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]
, which holds if both of these claims hold:

(C1)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\vdash\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]

(C2)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\vdash P\,\langle\!|\overline{{s}}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]

Note
D\vdash\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}\land\langle\!|G^{0}|\!\rangle[\overline{{s}}/\overline{{z}}]|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}},\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}^{0}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}]
, i.e.
D\vdash(\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}|\!\rangle^{-1}\land G^{0}[\langle\!|\overline{{s}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{z}}])[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}},\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}^{0}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}]
, by the IH on
G^{\prime\prime}
.
This implies the following, by
(And)
:

(IH1)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\vdash\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}},\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}^{0}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}]

(IH2)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\vdash G^{0}[\langle\!|\overline{{s}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{z}}][\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}},\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}^{0}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}]

Since
\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}|\!\rangle^{-1}
does not contain
\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}
or
\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}
,
IH1
gives us
C1
immediately.

We apply
(Ex)
to
IH2
for
D\vdash\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,G^{0}[\langle\!|\overline{{s}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{z}}][\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}},\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]
.
This is now an instance of the body of the original definite clause
(\forall\overline{{z}}.\,\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,G^{0}\Rightarrow P\,\overline{{z}})\in D
, so we apply
(Res)
to obtain
C2
.

–

Suppose the side condition is
(\forall v.\,\langle\!|\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,G^{0}|\!\rangle_{f\,\overline{{z}}}\Rightarrow P\,v)\in D_{\mathit{Th}}
with existential-free
G^{0}
from some
(\forall\overline{{z}}.\,\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,G^{0}\Rightarrow P\,(f\,\overline{{z}}))\in D
.
Then,
\langle\!|\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,G^{0}|\!\rangle_{f\,\overline{{z}}}
equals some
\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{z}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,\bigwedge_{y_{j}^{0}:\gamma^{0}_{j}\in\overline{{y}}}T_{\gamma^{0}_{j}}\,y_{j}^{0}\land(v=\langle\!|f\,\overline{{z}}|\!\rangle)\land\bigwedge_{z_{i}:\gamma^{1}_{i}\in\overline{{z}}}T_{\gamma^{1}_{i}}\,z_{i}\land\langle\!|G^{0}|\!\rangle
.

This gives us:

\displaystyle G^{\prime\prime}=\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{z}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}.\,

\displaystyle G_{\varphi}\land G_{\neg\varphi}\land\bigwedge_{y_{j}^{0}:\gamma^{0}_{j}\in\overline{{y}}}T_{\gamma^{0}_{j}}\,y_{j}^{0}\land(s_{1}=\langle\!|f\,\overline{{z}}|\!\rangle)

\displaystyle\land\;\bigwedge_{z_{i}:\gamma^{1}_{i}\in\overline{{z}}}T_{\gamma^{1}_{i}}\,z_{i}\land\langle\!|G^{0}|\!\rangle[s_{1}/v]

By the IH,
D\vdash\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}\land\langle\!|G^{0}|\!\rangle[s_{1}/v]|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\overline{{z}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{z}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}},\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}^{0}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}]
.
However,
v
does not occur in
\langle\!|G^{0}|\!\rangle
, so
\langle\!|G^{0}|\!\rangle[s_{1}/v]=\langle\!|G^{0}|\!\rangle
, which yields:

D\vdash(\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}|\!\rangle^{-1}\land G^{0})[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\overline{{z}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}^{0}\,\overline{{z}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}},\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}^{0}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}^{0}]

Compared with the IH in the previous case, only the substitution to
\overline{{z}}
is different;
instead of
\overline{{z}}
first being partially instantiated with
\langle\!|\overline{{s}}|\!\rangle^{-1}
(in the previous case),
\overline{{z}}
is directly fully instantiated by
\alpha
.

We know from Lemma
A.2
that
\alpha
assigns each
z_{i}\in\overline{{z}}
a closed term
\langle\!|t^{\prime}_{i}|\!\rangle
such that
t^{\prime}_{i}:\gamma^{1}_{i}
.
Furthermore,
\alpha
satisfies
H
and, by soundness of the HOCHC proof system, also
G^{\prime\prime}
.
In particular, this means
s_{1}
equals
\langle\!|f\,\overline{{z}}|\!\rangle
under
\alpha
, and
\langle\!|s_{1}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}]
equals
f\,\overline{{t^{\prime}}}
.

Since we set out to prove
D\vdash\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}\land P\,s_{1}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]
, it suffices to show

D\vdash\langle\!|G_{\neg\varphi}|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]\land P\,(f\,\overline{{t^{\prime}}})

We now proceed as in the previous case.

∎

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

Since
G_{\mathit{Th}}=\langle\!|G|\!\rangle
, it remains to show
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\langle\!|G|\!\rangle\twoheadrightarrow^{*}\top
implies
D\vdash G
.

Let
G=\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}.\,G_{\mathsf{var}}\land G_{A}
, so
G_{\mathit{Th}}=\langle\!|G|\!\rangle=\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}.\,G_{\varphi}\land\langle\!|G_{\mathsf{var}}\land G_{A}|\!\rangle
.
This gives us
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\exists\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}\,\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}.\,G_{\varphi}\land\langle\!|G_{\mathsf{var}}\land G_{A}|\!\rangle\twoheadrightarrow^{*}\top
for a
G_{\varphi}
and satisfying, type-respecting assignment
\alpha
.

By Replay Lemma
A.3
,
D\vdash\langle\!|\langle\!|G_{\mathsf{var}}\land G_{A}|\!\rangle|\!\rangle^{-1}[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]
, which is:

D\vdash(G_{\mathsf{var}}\land G_{A})[\langle\!|\alpha_{|{\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}}}|\!\rangle^{-1}/\overline{{y}}_{\mathsf{FO}}][\overline{{\mathsf{Univ}}}/\overline{{x}}_{\mathsf{HO}}]

Finally, we prove
Ds\vdash G
with
(Ex)
and conclude that the Figure
2
proof system is complete with respect to the standard semantics of HOL.
∎

A.2.
Elimination of predicate-type existentials

Corollary A.4.

Predicate-type existentials do not add expressive power to
HOMSL
(
\omega
).

Proof.

We prove that
D\vdash\exists x_{0}:\rho.\,G
if, and only if,
D\vdash G[\mho_{\rho}/x_{0}]
, relying on the reduction of provability in
HOMSL
(
\omega
) to provability in HOCHC with a decidable equational theory of first-order trees (see Theorem
2.4
, soundness and completeness of the proof system).

Direction
\Leftarrow
is immediate from
(Ex)
, so suppose
D\vdash\exists x_{0}:\rho.\,G
.
There exists a HOCHC proof:

\textstyle D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\exists x_{0}:\langle\!|\rho|\!\rangle.\,\langle\!|G|\!\rangle\twoheadrightarrow^{*}\exists x_{0}\,\overline{{x}}\,\overline{{y}}.\,\bigwedge_{i\in[1..n]}\varphi_{i}\bigwedge_{j\in[1..m]}x_{k_{j}}\,\overline{{M_{j}}}\twoheadrightarrow\top

with a satisfying assignment
\alpha
to the existentials
\overline{{y}}
with constructor types.
The existentials
x_{0}
and
\overline{{x}}
with predicate types are implicitly assigned the maximal element in the semantic domain of their respective types, which is captured by
\mho_{\langle\!|\rho^{\prime}|\!\rangle}
for type
\langle\!|\rho^{\prime}|\!\rangle
.

This means there is a proof
D_{\mathit{Th}}\vdash\langle\!|G|\!\rangle[\mho_{\langle\!|\rho|\!\rangle}/x_{0}]\twoheadrightarrow^{*}\top
.
Because
\langle\!|G|\!\rangle[\mho_{\langle\!|\rho|\!\rangle}/x_{0}]=\langle\!|G[\mho_{\rho}/x_{0}]|\!\rangle
, the final part of the proof of Theorem
2.4
gives us
D\vdash G[\mho_{\rho}/x_{0}]
.

We conclude that preprocessing
HOMSL
(
\omega
) can eliminate predicate-type existentials by instantiating them with universal relations.
∎

Appendix B
Supporting Material for Section
3

B.1.
Elimination of higher-order predicates

Note that the transformation on goal formulas has a straightforward retraction
(\!|\_|\!)^{-1}
which removes occurrences of
\mathsf{T}
and the
\#
.

Lemma B.1.

D\vdash_{\omega}G
implies
(\!|D|\!)\vdash_{1}(\!|G|\!)

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the proof object in the antecedent.

(T)
:

Clear by definition.

(Res)
:

In this case,
G
is of the form
A[\overline{{s}}/\overline{{y}}]
and there is a clause
(\forall\overline{{y}}.\,H\Rightarrow A)\in D
. By induction, we may assume that
(\!|D|\!)\vdash_{1}(\!|H[\overline{{s}}/\overline{{y}}]|\!)
. Note that, by definition,
(\!|H[\overline{{s}}/\overline{{y}}]|\!)=(\!|H|\!)[(\!|\overline{{s}}|\!)/\overline{{y}}]
and
(\!|A[\overline{{s}}/\overline{{y}}]|\!)=(\!|A|\!)[(\!|\overline{{s}}|\!)/\overline{{y}}]
. We consider two cases for
A
.

•:

If
A
is of shape
P\,(c\,\overline{{y}})
, then
(\!|A|\!)=\mathsf{T}\,(p^{\#}\,(c\,\overline{{y}}))
and we prove
(\!|A|\!)[(\!|\overline{{s}}|\!)/\overline{{y}}]=\mathsf{T}\,(p^{\#}\,(c\,(\!|\overline{{s}}|\!)))
by two applications of
(Res)
. The first uses the reflection clause from
(\!|D|\!)
to unwrap the
\mathsf{T}
predicate, obtaining
P\,(c\,(\!|\overline{{s}}|\!))
, and the second uses the transformed copy of the side premise to obtain
(\!|H|\!)[(\!|\overline{{s}}|\!)/\overline{{y}}]
, which is provable by the induction hypothesis.

•:

Otherwise,
A
is of the form
P\,\overline{{y}}
and
(\!|A|\!)=\mathsf{T}\,(p^{\#}\,\overline{{y}})
. We can prove
(\!|A|\!)[\overline{{(\!|s|\!)}}/\overline{{y}}]
by
(Res)
directly using the transformed copy of the side premise and the induction hypothesis.

(Ex)
:

In this case,
G
is of the form
\exists x.\,H
and
(\!|G|\!)=\exists x.\,(\!|H|\!)
. By induction, we may assume
(\!|H[t/x]|\!)
, which is synonymous with
(\!|H|\!)[(\!|t|\!)/x]
, is already provable. Therefore, the proof can be concluded using
(Ex)
.

(And)
:

Follows immediately from the induction hypotheses.

∎

We need to generalise a little bit in the converse direction.

Lemma B.2.

(\!|D|\!)\vdash_{1}G
implies
D\vdash_{\omega}(\!|G|\!)^{-1}

Proof.

The proof is by induction.

(T)
:

Clear by definition.

(Res)
:

In this case,
G
has the form
A[\overline{{s}}/\overline{{y}}]
and there is a clause
(\forall\overline{{y}}.\,H\Rightarrow A)\in(\!|D|\!)
. We distinguish cases on the form of
A
and the provenance of the side premise.

•:

If
A
is of the form
P\,(c\,\overline{{y}})
, then there is a clause
(\forall\overline{{y}}.\,(\!|H|\!)^{-1}\Rightarrow P\,(c\,\overline{{y}}))\in D
from which the side premise originates. From the induction hypothesis, we can assume that
D\vdash_{\omega}(\!|H[\overline{{s}}/\overline{{y}}]|\!)^{-1}
, which is, by definition,
D\vdash_{\omega}(\!|H|\!)^{-1}[(\!|\overline{{s}}|\!)^{-1}/\overline{{y]}}]
. Hence, we can conclude
(\!|G|\!)^{-1}
, namely
P\,(c\,(\!|\overline{{s}}|\!)^{-1})
, directly by
(Res)
.

•:

If
A
is of the form
\mathsf{T}\,(p^{\#}\,\overline{{y}})
and the side premise is not a reflection, then there is a clause
(\forall\overline{{y}}.\,(\!|H|\!)^{-1}\Rightarrow P\,\overline{{y}})\in D
. From the induction hypothesis we can assume
D\vdash_{\omega}(\!|H[\overline{{s}}/\overline{{y}}]|\!)^{-1}
, which is, by definition,
D\vdash_{\omega}(\!|H|\!)^{-1}[(\!|\overline{{s}}|\!)^{-1}/\overline{{y}}]
. We can conclude
(\!|G|\!)^{-1}
, namely
P\,(\!|\overline{{s}}|\!)^{-1}
, directly by
(Res)
.

•:

Otherwise the side premise is a reflection, has shape
\forall y.\,P\,y\Rightarrow\mathsf{T}\,(p^{\#}\,y)
, and
A
is of the form
\mathsf{T}\,(p^{\#}\,y)
. We may assume, by the induction hypothesis, that
D\vdash_{\omega}(\!|P\,s|\!)^{-1}
, which is
D\vdash_{\omega}P\,(\!|s|\!)^{-1}
by definition. But this is already
D\vdash_{\omega}(\!|G|\!)^{-1}
, so we are done.

(Ex)
:

In this case,
G
has the form
\exists x.\,H
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
t
such that
D\vdash_{\omega}(\!|H[t/x]|\!)^{-1}
. By definition, this means we have
D\vdash_{\omega}(\!|H|\!)^{-1}[(\!|t|\!)^{-1}/x]
and so we can conclude
D\vdash_{\omega}(\!|G|\!)^{-1}
, being
\exists x.\,(\!|H|\!)^{-1}
, immediately by
(Ex)
.

(And)
:

This case follows directly from the induction hypotheses.

∎

It now follows immediately from these lemmas that
D\vdash_{\omega}G
iff
(\!|D|\!)\vdash_{1}(\!|G|\!)
, proving Theorem
3.1
.

B.2.
Elimination of existentials

Lemma B.3.

For all terms
\exists_{\gamma}\,s
over signatures
{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}
and
{\Pi}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}
,
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\exists_{\gamma}\,s
if, and only if,
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash s\,t
for a closed
t:\gamma
.

Proof.

For
\Rightarrow
, let
\mathcal{T}
be an underlying proof tree of
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\exists_{\gamma}\,s
.
We prove
\mathcal{T}
contains
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash s\,t
for a closed
t:\gamma
, by induction on the number
m
of proof steps headed by any
\exists_{\gamma^{\prime}}
external to
s
.

•

If
m=1
, then, by our assumption of adequate signatures, there exists
c:\gamma\in\Sigma
such that a
\mathcal{T}
has shape:

\displaystyle{{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash s\,c}\mathstrut

(Res)

\displaystyle{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\mathsf{Comp}_{c}^{0,0}\,s\mathstrut

(Res)

\displaystyle{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\exists_{\gamma}\,s\mathstrut

•

If
m>1
, then
\mathcal{T}(1)={D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\mathsf{Comp}^{0,k}_{f}\,s
for a
k>0
and constructor
f:\gamma_{1}\to\dots\to\gamma_{k}\to\gamma
.
We apply the IH to the subproof rooted at node
111
(which clearly has
m^{\prime}_{1}<m
).

Now, if
k=1
, then we use
(Res)
with
\forall v\,x_{1}.\,v\,(f\,x_{1})\Rightarrow\mathsf{Comp}_{f}^{1,1}\,v\,x_{1}
to give us
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash s\,(f\,t_{1})
, as required.
Otherwise, we use
(Res)
with
\forall v\,x_{1}.\,\exists_{\gamma_{2}}\,(\mathsf{Comp}^{2,k}\,v\,x_{1})\Rightarrow\mathsf{Comp}^{1,k}_{f}\,v\,x_{1}
to give us
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\exists_{\gamma_{2}}\,(\mathsf{Comp}^{2,k}_{f}\,s\,t_{1})
to which we apply the IH again.

We use the IH a total of
k
times, once for each
\gamma_{i}
, for a proof tree of the following shape:

\displaystyle{{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash s\ (f\ t_{1}\dots t_{k})}\mathstrut

(Res)

\displaystyle{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\mathsf{Comp}_{f}^{k,k}\ s\ t_{1}\ \cdots\ t_{k}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\vdots\mathstrut

\displaystyle{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\mathsf{Comp}^{1,k}_{f}\,s\,t_{1}\mathstrut

(IH)

\displaystyle{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\exists_{\gamma_{1}}\,(\mathsf{Comp}^{1,k}_{f}\,s)\mathstrut

(Res)

\displaystyle{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\mathsf{Comp}^{0,k}_{f}\,s\mathstrut

(Res)

\displaystyle{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\exists_{\gamma}\,s\mathstrut

The direction
\Leftarrow
is a straightforward inverse of hereof, using induction on the number of symbols in
t
.
∎

Lemma B.4.

For all closed subformulas
G
of the original clauses,
D\vdash G
if and only if
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\Lbag G\Rbag
.

Proof.

In the forwards direction, we prove the following for all subformulas
G
of the original clauses with free variables
\overline{{z}}
:

D\vdash G[\overline{{t/z}}]\quad\text{implies}\quad{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\Lbag G\Rbag[\overline{{t/z}}]

The proof is by induction on
D\vdash G
.

(T)
:

Immediate.

(And)
:

Immediate from the induction hypothesis and the definition of the mapping.

(Ex)
:

In this case
G[\overline{{t/z}}]
is of shape
\exists x:\gamma.\,G^{\prime}[\overline{{t/z}}]
and
\Lbag G\Rbag=\exists_{\gamma}\,(\Lambda_{\gamma,\,G^{\prime}}\,\overline{{z}})
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\Lbag G^{\prime}\Rbag[\overline{{t/z}}][u/x]
for some closed
u
. Hence, it follows from
(Res)
that
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\Lambda_{\gamma,G^{\prime}}\,\overline{{t}}\,u
. Then, from Lemma
B.3
, we obtain
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\exists_{\gamma}\,(\Lambda_{\gamma,G^{\prime}}\,\overline{{t}})
, which is just
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\Lbag G\Rbag[\overline{{t/z}}]
as required.

(Res)
:

In this case
G[\overline{{t/z}}]
is of shape
A[\overline{{s/y}}]
and there is a clause
\forall\overline{{y}}.\ G^{\prime}\Rightarrow A
in
D
. Then there is a clause
\forall\overline{{y}}.\ \Lbag G^{\prime}\Rbag\Rightarrow A
in
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\Lbag G^{\prime}\Rbag[\overline{{s/y}}]
. Therefore, by
(Res)
, we get
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash A[\overline{{s/y}}]
. Since
G[\overline{{t/z}}]=A[\overline{{s/y}}]
with
A
the head of a clause, we have that
\Lbag G\Rbag=G
and hence our conclusion is already in the desired form.

For the backward direction, we prove the following for all subformulas
H^{\prime}
of the original clauses with free variables
\overline{{z}}
:

{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash H\quad\text{and}\quad H=\Lbag H^{\prime}\Rbag[\overline{{t/z}}]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{FV}(H^{\prime})=\overline{{z}}\quad\text{implies}\quad D\vdash H^{\prime}[\overline{{t/z}}]

The proof is by strong induction on
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash H
:

(T)
:

Immediate.

(And)
:

Immediate from the induction hypothesis and the definition of the mapping.

(Ex)
:

In this case,
H
is of shape
\exists x\mathord{:}\gamma.\ G
, but there is no
G^{\prime}
such that
\exists x\mathord{:}\gamma.\ G=\Lbag G^{\prime}\Rbag[\overline{{t/z}}]
. So the result is vacuously true.

(Res)
:

In this case,
H
is of shape
A[\overline{{s/y}}]
and we have
\forall\overline{{y}}.\ G\Rightarrow A\in{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}
. We analyse cases on the predicate that heads
A[\overline{{s/y}}]
(which is just the predicate that heads
A
). As in the previous case, this predicate cannot be
\Lambda_{\gamma,K}
or
\mathsf{Comp}_{f}^{i,j}
since neither of those heads a formula in the image of
\Lbag-\Rbag
.

•:

If the predicate is
\exists_{\gamma}
, we reason as follows. In this case, since
A
heads a clause in
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}
, it must be that
A
is
\exists_{\gamma}\,y
for some variable
y
. Therefore,
\overline{{s}}
is a singleton
s
. Suppose
A[\overline{{s/y}}]=\Lbag H^{\prime}\Rbag[\overline{{t/z}}]
for some
H^{\prime}
. Since there are no existential quantifiers in
A[\overline{{s/y}}]
and
A
is headed by
\exists_{\gamma}
,
\Lbag H^{\prime}\Rbag
must be of shape
\exists_{\gamma}\,(\Lambda_{\gamma,\,K}\,\overline{{z}})
for some
K
and
\overline{{z}}=\mathsf{FV}(K)
. Hence,
s=(\Lambda_{\gamma,\,K}\,\overline{{z}})
with
\overline{{z}}=\mathsf{FV}(K)
. Thus
A[\overline{{t/z}}]=\exists_{\gamma}\,(\Lambda_{\gamma,\,K}\,\overline{{t}})
and, since we know this is provable in the context
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}
, it follows from the proof of from Lemma
B.3
(although we did not state the lemma this way) that there is a closed term
u
and there is actually a subproof concluding
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash(\Lambda_{\gamma,\,K}\,\overline{{z}})\,u
. This subproof must be concluded using the rule
(Res)
with premise
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\Lbag K\Rbag[\overline{{t/z}}][u/x]
. Since this is a strict subproof of the original, we can apply the induction hypothesis to obtain
D\vdash K[\overline{{t/z}}][u/x]
. Then it follows from
(Ex)
that
D\vdash(\exists x:\gamma.\ K)[\overline{{t/z}}]
, as required.

•:

Otherwise the predicate is some
P
from
\Pi
(the old signature). In this case, the side premise comes from
\Lbag D\Rbag
and
G
of the side premise must be of shape
\Lbag G^{\prime}\Rbag
for some
G^{\prime}
from the original clauses with
\mathsf{FV}(G^{\prime})=\overline{{y}}
. Then, the induction hypothesis applies to
{D}_{\mathsf{\not\exists}}\vdash\Lbag G^{\prime}\Rbag[\overline{{s/y}}]
, so we can deduce
D\vdash G^{\prime}[\overline{{s/y}}]
. Then, by
(Res)
, we obtain
A[\overline{{s/y}}]
. Now, suppose that
A[\overline{{s/y}}]=\Lbag H^{\prime}\Rbag[\overline{{t/z}}]
as required by the statement. Since
A
is a head with predicate
P
, it can only be that
\Lbag H^{\prime}\Rbag=H^{\prime}
and so what we obtained is already in the correct form.

∎

Appendix C
Supporting Material for Section
4

Lemma C.1 (Reflexivity of implication).

V,\,\overline{{y}}\vdash(\forall\overline{{z}}.\,U\Rightarrow U)\triangleright^{*}\mathsf{true}

Lemma C.2 (Progress).

If
V,\,\overline{{z}}\vdash G[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]\triangleright H
then there is
K
and
U
such that:

\textit{(a)}\quad V,\,\overline{{y}}\overline{{z}}\vdash G\triangleright^{*}K\wedge U

and we can split

as
H_{1}\wedge H_{2}
so that both the following hold:

\textit{(b)}\quad V,\,\overline{{z}}\vdash U[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]\triangleright^{*}H_{1}\qquad\qquad\textit{(c)}\quad K[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]=H_{2}

and, moreover,
\mathsf{FV}(U)\subseteq\overline{{y}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the rewrite
V,\,\overline{{z}}\vdash G[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]\triangleright H
.

(Refl)
:

In this case,
H
is
\mathsf{true}
and
G[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]
is some
P\,x\in V
. Take

and (c) follows immediately. It must be that

for some variable
v
. We distinguish cases on
v
:

•:

If
v\in\overline{{y}}
then take
U
to be
P\ v
. Then (a) follows in 0 rewrite steps and (b) follows by
(Refl)
because
U[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]=P\ x
.

•:

Otherwise, take
U
to be
\mathsf{true}
. Then (a) follows by
(Refl)
and (b) is immediate.

(Step)
:

In this case,
G[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]=P\ (f\ \overline{{s}})
,

and there is a clause

. In general,

has shape

and so we distinguish cases on

:

If the head symbol of

is a variable

is of shape

,

\overline{{s_{1}s_{2}}}=\overline{{s}}

\overline{{x}}

\overline{{x_{1}}}\overline{{x_{2}}}

,

\displaystyle\triangleright\forall\overline{{x_{2}}}.\,U^{\prime}_{|{\overline{{x_{2}}}}}\Rightarrow U^{\prime}[\overline{{s_{1}}}\overline{{x_{2}}}/\overline{{x}}]

\displaystyle\triangleright^{*}(\forall\overline{{x_{2}}}.\,U^{\prime}_{|{\overline{{x_{2}}}}}\Rightarrow U^{\prime}_{|{\overline{{x_{1}}}}}[\overline{{s_{1}}}/\overline{{x_{1}}}])\wedge(\forall\overline{{x_{2}}}.\,U^{\prime}_{|{\overline{{x_{2}}}}}\Rightarrow U^{\prime}_{|{\overline{{x_{2}}}}})

with the latter conjunct rewriting to
\mathsf{true}
by the reflexivity of implication, leaving

Otherwise,

(Assm)

(AndL)
,
(AndR)
:

Since both are similar, we only show the former. In this case
G[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]
has shape

has shape

and we have that

,

and we may split

and

and both (b) and (c) follow straightforwardly from the induction hypothesis.

(Imp)
:

In this case,
G[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]
has shape
\forall\overline{{x}}.\,W\Rightarrow G^{\prime}[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]
and
H
has shape
\forall\overline{{x}}.\,W\Rightarrow H^{\prime}[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]
and we have
V,\overline{{z}}\vdash G^{\prime}[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]\triangleright H^{\prime}
. Note that, as
W
is an automaton body it only contain
\overline{{x}}
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that there are
K^{\prime}
and

and that we can split

as

, such that the required properties hold. Then take

,

and

. Then (a) follows because
U
can only contain the
\overline{{y}}
as free variables, we can apply scoping to deduce:

V,\overline{{y}}\overline{{z}}\vdash\forall\overline{{x}}.\,W\Rightarrow G^{\prime}\triangleright\forall\overline{{x}}.\,W\Rightarrow K^{\prime}\wedge U^{\prime}\triangleright(\forall\overline{{x}}.\,W\Rightarrow K^{\prime})\wedge U^{\prime}

and properties (b) and (c) follow from the induction hypothesis.

(ImpAnd)
:

In this case,
G[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]
has shape
\forall\overline{{x}}.\,U^{\prime}\Rightarrow G_{2}[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]\wedge G_{3}[\overline{{u}}/\overline{{y}}]
. Set

,

,
